# Daily Summary: 2025-06-28

### Multi-lingual Functional Evaluation for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20793v1)
- **Authors**: Victor Ojewale, Inioluwa Deborah Raji, Suresh Venkatasubramanian
- **Abstract**: Multi-lingual competence in large language models is often evaluated via static data benchmarks such as Belebele, M-MMLU and M-GSM. However, these evaluations often fail to provide an adequate understanding of the practical performance and robustness of models across multi-lingual settings. In response, we create multi-lingual functional benchmarks -- Cross-Lingual Grade School Math Symbolic (CL-GSM Symbolic) and Cross-Lingual Instruction-Following Eval (CL-IFEval)-- by translating existing functional benchmark templates from English to five additional languages that span the range of resources available for NLP: French, Spanish, Hindi, Arabic and Yoruba. Our results reveal that some static multi-lingual benchmarks capture functional performance much more closely than others (i.e. across models, there is a 24%, 17% and 18% decrease in performance between M-GSM and CL-GSM Symbolic in English, French and Spanish respectively; similarly there's a 15 - 24% performance drop across languages between Belebele and CL-IFEval, and only a 0.5% to 3% performance drop between M-MMLU and CL-IFEval). Similarly, we find that model robustness across languages varies significantly, with certain languages (eg. Arabic, English) being the most consistently well performing across evaluation iterations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of static multi-lingual benchmarks for evaluating large language models (LLMs). It argues that these benchmarks fail to capture the practical performance and robustness of models across different languages. To address this, the authors create multi-lingual functional benchmarks by translating existing English "functional" evaluation datasets (IFEval and GSM-Symbolic) into five additional languages: French, Spanish, Hindi, Arabic, and Yoruba.  The paper then compares performance on these new benchmarks against existing static benchmarks, revealing inconsistencies in model performance and robustness across languages. It finds that some static benchmarks are better indicators of functional performance than others, and that robustness varies significantly across languages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the creation and application of *functional*, rather than static, multi-lingual benchmarks.  While translation of existing benchmarks is common, the focus on functional benchmarks and subsequent analysis of the discrepancy between functional and static benchmark performance is a valuable contribution. The specific choice of languages to represent a spectrum of resource availability also adds value.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a crucial gap in the current evaluation landscape of multi-lingual LLMs. Demonstrating that models can perform well on static benchmarks while exhibiting weaknesses in more realistic, functionally-oriented tasks has significant implications. It suggests that current evaluation methods may overestimate true multi-lingual competence and robustness.  This has practical implications for the deployment of LLMs in real-world, multi-lingual settings where models will need to generalize to unseen variations within a language and be robust to linguistic differences.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:**  The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing multi-lingual evaluation methods.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors provide a sound methodology for creating functional benchmarks through template-based translation. They performed spot checks to verify translation quality.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper conducts a thorough analysis of model performance across different benchmarks, languages, and instruction types.
    *   **Relevance:** The paper addresses a relevant and important issue in the development of multi-lingual LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Translation Quality Concerns:** While the authors performed spot checks on translation quality, the use of automatic translation tools (Google Translate) introduces potential for inaccuracies, especially for low-resource languages like Yoruba. Human-curated translations would strengthen the validity of the functional benchmarks.
    *   **Limited Model Scope:** The primary focus is on open-weight models, limiting comparison with commercial models which might offer improved multi-lingual capabilities.
    *   **Complexity of the Datasets:** Both GSM-Symbolic and IFEval datasets are relatively complex. It would benefit from a simplified or more interpretable functional benchmark for multilingual assessment.
    *   **Limited Error Analysis:** A deeper dive into the specific types of errors made by the models on the functional benchmarks would be useful. What kinds of instructions or mathematical problems do models struggle with most in specific languages?

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of multi-lingual LLM evaluation by promoting the use of functional benchmarks. The identified inconsistencies between static and functional performance could encourage researchers to develop more robust and reliable evaluation methods. The paper's analysis of robustness across languages could also inform the development of targeted strategies for improving model performance in low-resource languages.

*   **Justification of Score:**  While the paper makes a valuable contribution, the limitations in translation quality and model scope slightly reduce its overall impact. Also, While the contrast between static and functional benchmark performance is important to highlight, the particular choice of static data is a bit arbitrary. Belebele for instance is know to have various weaknesses. With more rigorous data collection, the results and their impact would be significantly improved.

Score: 7

### The Ideation-Execution Gap: Execution Outcomes of LLM-Generated versus Human Research Ideas
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20803v1)
- **Authors**: Chenglei Si, Tatsunori Hashimoto, Diyi Yang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown promise in accelerating the scientific research pipeline. A key capability for this process is the ability to generate novel research ideas, and prior studies have found settings in which LLM-generated research ideas were judged as more novel than human-expert ideas. However, a good idea should not simply appear to be novel, it should also result in better research after being executed. To test whether AI-generated ideas lead to better research outcomes, we conduct an execution study by recruiting 43 expert researchers to execute randomly-assigned ideas, either written by experts or generated by an LLM. Each expert spent over 100 hours implementing the idea and wrote a 4-page short paper to document the experiments. All the executed projects are then reviewed blindly by expert NLP researchers. Comparing the review scores of the same ideas before and after execution, the scores of the LLM-generated ideas decrease significantly more than expert-written ideas on all evaluation metrics (novelty, excitement, effectiveness, and overall; p < 0.05), closing the gap between LLM and human ideas observed at the ideation stage. When comparing the aggregated review scores from the execution study, we even observe that for many metrics there is a flip in rankings where human ideas score higher than LLM ideas. This ideation-execution gap highlights the limitations of current LLMs in generating truly effective research ideas and the challenge of evaluating research ideas in the absence of execution outcomes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "The Ideation-Execution Gap: Execution Outcomes of LLM-Generated versus Human Research Ideas":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether the perceived novelty of research ideas generated by Large Language Models (LLMs) translates into better research outcomes after execution.  The authors conducted a controlled experiment where 43 expert researchers were tasked with executing randomly assigned research ideas, some generated by LLMs and others by human experts.  The researchers spent over 100 hours implementing the ideas and documenting their experiments in short papers. These papers were then blindly reviewed by NLP experts.  The study compared review scores before and after execution, finding that the scores of LLM-generated ideas decreased significantly more than those of human-written ideas. In some cases, after execution, human-generated ideas were even ranked higher than LLM-generated ones, reversing the initial perception of LLM novelty. The paper highlights this "ideation-execution gap" and underscores the limitations of current LLMs in generating truly effective research ideas, as well as the challenges in evaluating research ideas without considering their execution outcomes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core concept of exploring the "ideation-execution gap" in LLM-generated research ideas is **moderately novel**. While prior works have evaluated LLM-generated content, this study specifically examines the translation of perceived novelty into practical research outcomes. This provides a valuable perspective that moves beyond simple subjective judgements of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper is **significant** because it challenges the assumption that LLMs, which are increasingly used to augment scientific research, are inherently capable of generating high-impact research ideas. The finding that LLM-generated ideas, despite often being initially perceived as novel, fare worse upon execution has important implications for how we integrate AI into the scientific research pipeline.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The controlled experiment involving expert researchers, blind reviews, and pre- and post-execution evaluations strengthens the validity of the findings. The substantial time investment by the executing researchers (over 100 hours each) lends credence to the thoroughness of the execution.
    *   **Quantitative and Qualitative Analysis:** Combining quantitative review scores with qualitative analysis of reviewer comments provides a nuanced understanding of the reasons behind the observed ideation-execution gap.
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly defines the research question and sets out a straightforward methodology to address it.
    *   **Large-Scale Execution Study:** The study is rare for its large scale, especially considering the resource and time-intensive process of executing research ideas.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Ideas:** The LLM and human ideas used in the study were intentionally scoped to be feasible to execute within a three-month period. This constraint might limit the generalizability of the findings to more ambitious or long-term research projects.
    *   **Subjectivity of Reviews:** Although blind, the review process inherently involves subjectivity.  While attempts were made to standardize the evaluation, individual reviewer biases could still influence the results.
    *   **Specific to NLP Research:** The study focuses on NLP research ideas, which may not be representative of other scientific disciplines. The feasibility and impact of ideas might differ significantly in fields with different resource requirements or evaluation paradigms.
    *   **Statistical Power:** While the study has a relatively large sample size, it is not entirely conclusive to directly confirm that human ideas differ significantly from AI ideas in the execution scores.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence how researchers approach the use of LLMs in scientific discovery. By highlighting the "ideation-execution gap," it encourages a more critical assessment of AI-generated ideas and a greater emphasis on validation through experimentation. The work also suggests that evaluation metrics for research ideas should consider factors beyond initial perceived novelty.

**Overall Score:** 7.5

**Justification:** The paper presents a well-designed and executed study that reveals a significant limitation in the current application of LLMs to scientific research. The finding of the "ideation-execution gap" is valuable and prompts further research into how LLMs can generate more effective research ideas and how we can best evaluate the true potential of any proposed idea. While the study's scope is limited to NLP research and constrained by the feasibility of execution, the rigor of the methodology and the significance of the findings warrant a high score. The limitations and areas for future research are clearly articulated, which contributes to the paper's value as a foundation for future investigations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Poster: Enhancing GNN Robustness for Network Intrusion Detection via Agent-based Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20806v1)
- **Authors**: Zhonghao Zhan, Huichi Zhou, Hamed Haddadi
- **Abstract**: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) show great promise for Network Intrusion Detection Systems (NIDS), particularly in IoT environments, but suffer performance degradation due to distribution drift and lack robustness against realistic adversarial attacks. Current robustness evaluations often rely on unrealistic synthetic perturbations and lack demonstrations on systematic analysis of different kinds of adversarial attack, which encompass both black-box and white-box scenarios. This work proposes a novel approach to enhance GNN robustness and generalization by employing Large Language Models (LLMs) in an agentic pipeline as simulated cybersecurity expert agents. These agents scrutinize graph structures derived from network flow data, identifying and potentially mitigating suspicious or adversarially perturbed elements before GNN processing. Our experiments, using a framework designed for realistic evaluation and testing with a variety of adversarial attacks including a dataset collected from physical testbed experiments, demonstrate that integrating LLM analysis can significantly improve the resilience of GNN-based NIDS against challenges, showcasing the potential of LLM agent as a complementary layer in intrusion detection architectures.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper.

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) for Network Intrusion Detection Systems (NIDS) concerning distribution drift and vulnerability to adversarial attacks. The authors propose a novel approach to enhance GNN robustness by integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) as simulated cybersecurity expert agents. These LLM agents analyze network flow graph data, identifying and potentially mitigating suspicious or adversarially perturbed elements *before* the GNN processes it. The paper presents experimental results demonstrating that this LLM-augmented approach improves the resilience of GNN-based NIDS against realistic attacks, particularly node injection.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

This paper presents a novel and promising approach to addressing two significant challenges in GNN-based NIDS: distribution drift and adversarial robustness. The core idea of employing LLMs as simulated cybersecurity expert agents to pre-process network flow data before GNN analysis is quite interesting and offers a unique way to improve performance and resilience.  Here's a breakdown of strengths, weaknesses, and impact:

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The combination of LLMs and GNNs for NIDS is relatively unexplored. The idea of using LLMs as "expert filters" to enhance GNN robustness specifically targets a critical weakness of GNNs in real-world deployments.
*   **Addressing Realistic Attacks:** The paper acknowledges and attempts to address the limitations of existing robustness evaluations that rely on unrealistic synthetic perturbations. The use of a unified dataset to simulate distribution drift and the simulation of problem-space black-box attacks like node injection are steps in the right direction.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The paper provides experimental results that demonstrate the effectiveness of the LLM-based mitigation strategy, particularly in identifying and flagging malicious nodes injected into the network graph.  The inclusion of different LLM models (GPT-40, LLaMA, Claude) provides a good baseline analysis of LLM model performance in terms of network graph mitigation.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper has clear practical implications for building more robust and reliable NIDS. The suggested approach could potentially improve the performance of GNN-based NIDS in real-world environments where traffic patterns are constantly evolving and adversarial attacks are common.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Attack Scenarios:** While node injection is a relevant attack, the paper would be strengthened by evaluating the LLM mitigation strategy against a wider range of adversarial attacks and drift scenarios. The node injection is still a relatively simple attack scenario and the results may not generalize to more sophisticated attacks.
*   **Scalability and Cost Considerations:** The paper does not adequately address the scalability and cost implications of using LLMs for real-time network traffic analysis. LLMs can be computationally expensive, and the overhead of LLM analysis could potentially introduce latency and reduce the overall throughput of the NIDS.
*   **Prompt Engineering Dependency:** The performance of the LLM-based mitigation strategy is likely to be highly dependent on the quality of the prompts used to guide the LLM's analysis. The paper does not provide sufficient details about the prompt engineering process, and it is unclear how the prompts were optimized for different attack scenarios. Furthermore, no sensitivity analysis is made on the prompting, or how prompt injection attack might also impede the LLM mitigation strategy.
*   **Limited Data on LLM decisioning:** Although the experiment section shows how the LLM models are correctly flagging potential malicious nodes in the graph, it remains unknown how the LLM made that decision. A decision explanation is needed to further validate the reasoning as to why a node is considered malicious in the network graph.
*   **Generality Limitations:** Although the authors use a combined dataset to avoid overfitting on any single dataset, the scope of the combined datasets is still somewhat limited and do not fully reflect the types of network intrusion that a real-world network might encounter.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its novel approach to enhancing GNN robustness in NIDS using LLMs. The paper raises awareness about the limitations of standard GNN evaluations and proposes a promising direction for future research.  It highlights the potential of LLMs to augment GNNs in security applications and encourages further investigation into this area.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, I assign this paper a score of **7**.  While the idea is innovative and the preliminary results are encouraging, the paper has limitations in terms of the breadth of evaluated attack scenarios, a lack of scalability analysis, and insufficient details on prompt engineering and LLM explainability.  The potential impact on the field is significant if these limitations are addressed in future work. The approach offers a promising avenue for building more robust and adaptable NIDS. However, further research is needed to fully assess the practical feasibility and generalizability of the proposed approach.
Score: 7
