### RecCoT: Enhancing Recommendation via Chain-of-Thought
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21032v1)
- **Authors**: Shuo Yang, Jiangxia Cao, Haipeng Li, Yuqi Mao, Shuchao Pang
- **Abstract**: In real-world applications, users always interact with items in multiple aspects, such as through implicit binary feedback (e.g., clicks, dislikes, long views) and explicit feedback (e.g., comments, reviews). Modern recommendation systems (RecSys) learn user-item collaborative signals from these implicit feedback signals as a large-scale binary data-streaming, subsequently recommending other highly similar items based on users' personalized historical interactions. However, from this collaborative-connection perspective, the RecSys does not focus on the actual content of the items themselves but instead prioritizes higher-probability signals of behavioral co-occurrence among items. Consequently, under this binary learning paradigm, the RecSys struggles to understand why a user likes or dislikes certain items. To alleviate it, some works attempt to utilize the content-based reviews to capture the semantic knowledge to enhance recommender models. However, most of these methods focus on predicting the ratings of reviews, but do not provide a human-understandable explanation.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper and will provide a summary, critical evaluation, and a score with justification.

**Summary:**

The paper "RecCoT: Enhancing Recommendation via Chain-of-Thought" proposes a novel framework to improve recommendation systems by incorporating semantic knowledge extracted from user reviews using a Chain-of-Thought (CoT) approach. The RecCoT framework consists of two main stages: (1) Generating interpretable CoTs from reviews using a larger LLM fine-tuned with a frequency-aware reward policy to address the long-tail rating distribution problem, and (2) Aligning semantic embeddings via a smaller LLM that is fine-tuned on the CoTs and reviews for faster semantic embedding to be later used in RecSys.  The generated semantic embeddings are then utilized in a downstream recommendation model. The authors conduct experiments on Amazon datasets and demonstrate significant improvements over state-of-the-art methods, highlighting the benefits of the reasoning ability and interpretable semantic knowledge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using CoT for recommendation systems is interesting. The authors introduce a frequency-aware reward policy which is novel to handle the long-tail distribution problem in reviews which is an interesting contribution. The two-stage approach (larger LLM for CoT generation and smaller LLM for embedding alignment) is a reasonable design choice for efficiency. Caching the embeddings after aligning via LLMs is not novel on it's own.

*   **Significance:** The improvements over SOTA methods are promising, suggesting that incorporating interpretable semantic knowledge through CoT can indeed enhance recommendation accuracy. The interpretable CoT also offers the possibility of understanding user preferences and item characteristics better, which can lead to more transparent and explainable recommendations. The paper addresses a clear problem - lack of semantic knowledge and explainability in traditional RecSys - and proposes a plausible solution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The frequency-aware reward policy is a well-motivated approach.
    *   The two-stage LLM architecture balances reasoning power and efficiency.
    *   Empirical results demonstrate performance gains on multiple datasets.
    *   The case studies provide some qualitative insights into the generated CoTs.
    *   Addressing the long-tail rating distribution is critical.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper does not clearly address cost. Caching only handles inference, the cost of using 2 LLMs during training is substantial.
    *   The evaluation could be more rigorous. More explanation of the compared baseline methods (BiGI, RGCL, LoID) is lacking. A comparison to a "vanilla" LLM embedding approach (e.g., directly using sentence embeddings without CoT) would strengthen the argument for the necessity of CoT.
    *   The qualitative examples, while illustrative, are limited. A more in-depth analysis of the quality of the generated CoTs (e.g., measuring coherence, completeness, etc.) is needed.
    *   The paper could be improved through ablation studies to show the contribution of individual model components more clearly.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to enhancing recommendation systems by leveraging Chain-of-Thought reasoning. The frequency-aware reward mechanism is a key contribution. While the results demonstrate improvements over existing methods, the evaluation could be more thorough (e.g. comparing against baselines). The lack of detailed qualitative analysis and cost analysis also detracts from the overall impact. While the ideas here may well form the base for more valuable work in the future, this particular work has some limitations and the results could be better validated.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### BLOCKS: Blockchain-supported Cross-Silo Knowledge Sharing for Efficient LLM Services
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21033v1)
- **Authors**: Zhaojiacheng Zhou, Hongze Liu, Shijing Yuan, Hanning Zhang, Jiong Lou, Chentao Wu, Jie Li
- **Abstract**: The hallucination problem of Large Language Models (LLMs) has increasingly drawn attention. Augmenting LLMs with external knowledge is a promising solution to address this issue. However, due to privacy and security concerns, a vast amount of downstream task-related knowledge remains dispersed and isolated across various "silos," making it difficult to access. To bridge this knowledge gap, we propose a blockchain-based external knowledge framework that coordinates multiple knowledge silos to provide reliable foundational knowledge for large model retrieval while ensuring data security. Technically, we distill knowledge from local data into prompts and execute transactions and records on the blockchain. Additionally, we introduce a reputation mechanism and cross-validation to ensure knowledge quality and provide incentives for participation. Furthermore, we design a query generation framework that provides a direct API interface for large model retrieval. To evaluate the performance of our proposed framework, we conducted extensive experiments on various knowledge sources. The results demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves efficient LLM service knowledge sharing in blockchain environments.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper and will provide a summary and critical evaluation, including a novelty/significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper "BLOCKS: Blockchain-supported Cross-Silo Knowledge Sharing for Efficient LLM Services" proposes a blockchain-based framework for coordinating multiple knowledge silos to provide reliable foundational knowledge for large language model (LLM) retrieval while ensuring data security. The system addresses challenges in incentivizing knowledge sharing, ensuring quality of service (QoS), and maintaining security in a decentralized environment. BLOCKS distills knowledge into prompts recorded on a blockchain, uses a reputation mechanism and cross-validation for quality control, and introduces a caching mechanism (PROCache) for efficient retrieval. The paper presents experimental results demonstrating the framework's efficiency in enabling secure LLM service knowledge sharing in blockchain environments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a relevant and timely problem:** The paper tackles the critical issue of knowledge silos hindering LLM performance. Augmenting LLMs with external knowledge is widely recognized as crucial, and the problem of distributed knowledge across silos is a real challenge.
*   **Blockchain-based solution:** The use of blockchain for secure and incentivized knowledge sharing is a logical and promising approach. The paper justifies the need for blockchain by highlighting its transparency, immutability, and traceability properties.
*   **Comprehensive design:** The paper outlines a detailed system design, including the roles of different participants (knowledge providers, LLM servers, validators), a reputation mechanism, and a caching strategy.
*   **PROCache is a valuable addition:** The proposed PROCache caching mechanism addresses the throughput limitations of blockchain and the potential for malicious cache pollution.
*   **Experimental validation:** The paper presents experimental results across diverse knowledge sources, demonstrating the effectiveness of the framework.
*   **Consideration of Prompt Injection:** Addresses prompt injection attacks, one of the main concerns with using outside knowledge to augment LLMs.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the paper combines several existing technologies (blockchain, reputation systems, caching), its novelty lies primarily in the *application* of these technologies to the specific problem of LLM knowledge sharing.  The core components themselves aren't radically new.
*   **Scalability Concerns:** Although the paper mentions performance improvements through caching, the inherent scalability limitations of blockchain itself could still be a bottleneck. The experiments do not appear to test the framework at a very large scale in terms of the number of participating silos or the volume of transactions.
*   **Complexity:** The system introduces significant complexity. Deploying and managing a decentralized knowledge-sharing platform with blockchain, smart contracts, and reputation systems can be challenging in practice.
*   **Experimental Detail:** Details of the experimental setup need to be more robust. Providing information like the specific LLMs used in tests would also be helpful to improve the soundness of the analysis.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its potential to unlock valuable knowledge currently trapped in silos, which can greatly enhance LLM performance in various domains. However, the practical impact will depend on the successful deployment and adoption of the framework, which could be hindered by scalability and complexity issues.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-motivated and designed solution to a relevant problem. However, the incremental nature of its novelty, combined with scalability concerns and complexity, limits its overall impact. It's a solid contribution but not a groundbreaking one.

### DidSee: Diffusion-Based Depth Completion for Material-Agnostic Robotic Perception and Manipulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21034v1)
- **Authors**: Wenzhou Lyu, Jialing Lin, Wenqi Ren, Ruihao Xia, Feng Qian, Yang Tang
- **Abstract**: Commercial RGB-D cameras often produce noisy, incomplete depth maps for non-Lambertian objects. Traditional depth completion methods struggle to generalize due to the limited diversity and scale of training data. Recent advances exploit visual priors from pre-trained text-to-image diffusion models to enhance generalization in dense prediction tasks. However, we find that biases arising from training-inference mismatches in the vanilla diffusion framework significantly impair depth completion performance. Additionally, the lack of distinct visual features in non-Lambertian regions further hinders precise prediction. To address these issues, we propose \textbf{DidSee}, a diffusion-based framework for depth completion on non-Lambertian objects. First, we integrate a rescaled noise scheduler enforcing a zero terminal signal-to-noise ratio to eliminate signal leakage bias. Second, we devise a noise-agnostic single-step training formulation to alleviate error accumulation caused by exposure bias and optimize the model with a task-specific loss. Finally, we incorporate a semantic enhancer that enables joint depth completion and semantic segmentation, distinguishing objects from backgrounds and yielding precise, fine-grained depth maps. DidSee achieves state-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmarks, demonstrates robust real-world generalization, and effectively improves downstream tasks such as category-level pose estimation and robotic grasping.Project page: https://wenzhoulyu.github.io/DidSee/
- **Summary**: The paper "DidSee: Diffusion-Based Depth Completion for Material-Agnostic Robotic Perception and Manipulation" addresses the problem of depth completion for non-Lambertian objects, which is crucial for robotic perception and manipulation.  Commercial RGB-D cameras often produce noisy and incomplete depth maps for such objects, hindering reliable robot operations. The paper proposes a diffusion-based framework, DidSee, designed to overcome these challenges by mitigating biases inherent in standard diffusion models and incorporating a semantic enhancer to improve object-background distinction.  Specifically, DidSee introduces a rescaled noise scheduler to eliminate signal leakage bias, a noise-agnostic single-step training formulation to alleviate exposure bias, and a semantic enhancer for joint depth completion and semantic segmentation.  The authors demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmark datasets and show improved performance in downstream tasks like category-level pose estimation and robotic grasping.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper presents a well-structured approach to addressing a specific and important problem in robotics: depth completion for challenging objects. While the individual components (diffusion models, semantic segmentation) are not entirely novel in isolation, their combination and adaptation within the DidSee framework exhibits notable novelty.

**Strengths:**

*   **Targeted Problem:** The paper tackles a very practical problem: noisy depth data from non-Lambertian objects, a pervasive issue in real-world robotics scenarios.
*   **Bias Mitigation:** Identifying and addressing signal leakage and exposure biases in the diffusion model is a significant contribution. These biases are often overlooked in generic diffusion applications but become critical in dense prediction tasks like depth completion. The rescaled noise scheduler and single-step training formulation directly address these weaknesses.
*   **Semantic Enhancer:** Integrating semantic information improves the model's ability to distinguish objects from backgrounds, leading to more accurate and fine-grained depth maps. This directly addresses the ambiguity in visual features presented by non-Lambertian surfaces.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results on multiple benchmark datasets, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance.  The ablation studies clearly show the contribution of each component of the proposed framework. Moreover, the improvements demonstrated in downstream tasks highlight the practical utility of DidSee.
*   **Real-world Generalization:**  The paper emphasizes and demonstrates robust generalization to real-world scenarios, a key requirement for robotic applications.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Stable Diffusion:** The method relies heavily on the Stable Diffusion model, which, while powerful, can be computationally expensive. The paper acknowledges this limitation in the conclusion but does not offer specific solutions beyond vague references to future work to enhance efficiency. Also, this reliance makes it hard to say how generalizable the proposed approach is to the use of alternative diffusion models.
*   **Incremental Improvements:** While the overall framework is effective, it largely combines and adapts existing techniques rather than introducing fundamentally new concepts. The novelty lies in the specific combination and tuning for the depth completion task.
*   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** The paper focuses primarily on empirical results and lacks a deep theoretical analysis of why the proposed techniques are effective, particularly concerning the bias mitigation strategies. While the effects are evident experimentally, a more rigorous theoretical grounding would strengthen the paper.

**Significance:**

The paper has the potential to be significantly impactful in the field of robotics. By providing a robust and accurate depth completion method for non-Lambertian objects, DidSee can enable more reliable and versatile robotic manipulation and perception systems.  The clear experimental validation and demonstration of real-world generalization further enhance its practical relevance. However, more work is necessary to reduce the computational overhead.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper is a solid contribution to the field of robotic perception. The identification and mitigation of biases within the diffusion model specifically for depth completion are notable advancements. The semantic enhancer also improves the realism and precision of the restored depth maps. The comprehensive experimental validation, including downstream tasks, demonstrates the practical value of the work.

However, the reliance on a computationally expensive pre-trained diffusion model is a significant limitation. Also, while the combination of techniques is novel, the individual components are not groundbreaking in themselves. Finally, a deeper theoretical analysis could strengthen the paper. As such, while highly valuable and practically impactful, it lacks the transformative novelty required for a higher score.

### Little By Little: Continual Learning via Self-Activated Sparse Mixture-of-Rank Adaptive Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21035v1)
- **Authors**: Haodong Lu, Chongyang Zhao, Jason Xue, Lina Yao, Kristen Moore, Dong Gong
- **Abstract**: Continual learning (CL) with large pre-trained models is challenged by catastrophic forgetting and task interference. Existing LoRA-based Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) approaches mitigate forgetting by assigning and freezing task-specific adapters, but suffer from interference, redundancy, and ambiguous routing due to coarse adapter-level selection. However, this design introduces three key challenges: 1) Interference: Activating full LoRA experts per input leads to subspace interference and prevents selective reuse of useful components across tasks. 2) Redundancy: Newly added experts often duplicate or contradict existing knowledge due to unnecessary activation of unrelated ranks and insufficient reuse of relevant ones. 3) Ambiguity: Overlapping features across tasks confuse the router, resulting in unstable expert assignments. As more experts accumulate, earlier task routing degrades, accelerating forgetting. We propose MoRA, a Mixture-of-Rank Adaptive learning approach with self-activated and sparse rank activation for CL. Unlike mixing multiple low-rank matrices, MoRA decomposes each rank-r update into r rank-1 components, each treated as an independent expert, enabling fine-grained mixture of rank-1 expert utilization while mitigating interference and redundancy. To avoid ambiguous routing, we propose that each rank-1 expert can infer its own relevance via intermediate activations. Coupled with our proposed rank pruning and activation budgets, MoRA adaptively selects a sparse mixture of ranks per input. We validate MoRA on continual learning tasks with CLIP and large language models (LLMs), analyzing both in-domain learning and out-of-domain forgetting/generalization during fine-tuning. MoRA shows significant effectiveness on enhancing CL with PTMs, and improving generalization while mitigating forgetting.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MoRA (Mixture-of-Rank Adaptive learning), a novel approach to continual learning (CL) using large pre-trained models (PTMs).  MoRA addresses the limitations of LoRA-based Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) methods, which suffer from interference, redundancy, and ambiguous routing.  Instead of activating full LoRA experts, MoRA decomposes each rank-r update into r rank-1 components, treating each as an individual expert.  These rank-1 experts use a self-activation mechanism based on intermediate activations to determine their relevance to the input, leading to a sparse mixture of ranks.  Combined with rank pruning and activation budgets, MoRA adaptively selects a sparse mixture of ranks per input, mitigating interference and redundancy.  The authors validate MoRA on continual learning tasks with CLIP and LLMs, showing improved performance in in-domain learning and out-of-domain forgetting/generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of decomposing LoRA updates into rank-1 components and using self-activation for sparse mixture is novel. Prior MoE-LoRA methods operated at a coarser granularity (adapter-level), while MoRA's fine-grained rank-level operation provides more flexibility and specialization. The self-activation mechanism, eliminating the need for a separate router, is also a significant contribution. The novelty also lies in the perspective of framing low-rank updates to weight matrices as inserting new memories using keys and values, and relating the memory's relevance using the input.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in continual learning with large models: catastrophic forgetting and interference. MoRA demonstrates strong empirical results on several benchmarks (X-TAIL, MTIL, and LLM continual learning), suggesting its potential to significantly improve CL performance in real-world scenarios. The increased efficiency due to sparse activation is also practically important. The demonstrated improvements in generalization, alongside forgetting mitigation, are particularly significant as it suggests that MoRA enables adaptation without compromising the pre-trained knowledge.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing MoE-LoRA methods.
    *   **Technically Sound:** The proposed method is well-described and grounded in a solid theoretical understanding of low-rank adaptation and MoE. The memory view of low rank adaptation is insightful.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper provides extensive experimental validation across diverse tasks and models.  Ablation studies are used to demonstrate the effectiveness of the different components of MoRA.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation considers both in-domain learning and out-of-domain generalization, offering a holistic view of the method's capabilities.
    *   **Thorough Implementation Details:** Provides settings for models, benchmarks, and training details for reproducability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While the paper presents a good intuition and empirical evidence, a more formal theoretical analysis of why MoRA works could strengthen the contribution. For example, bounding the interference or analyzing the convergence properties.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The method introduces several hyperparameters (temperature, activation budget, threshold), and while ablation studies are performed, a more systematic analysis of their impact could be valuable, and possible automated method of setting these values.
    *   **Computational overhead:** The paper touches on its computational savings due to sparse activation in reference to other LoRA based models but a deeper dive into actual training time with its additional layer decomposition should be included.
    *   **Limited Comparison:** Some existing methods in LoRA based models are not mentioned and discussed when doing comparisons to LoRA baseline.

*   **Potential Impact:** MoRA has the potential to influence future research in continual learning, particularly for large models. The idea of fine-grained rank-level mixture and self-activation could inspire new architectures and algorithms. The improved generalization results also open up new possibilities for adapting PTMs to new tasks without sacrificing their pre-trained knowledge.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite the minor weaknesses, the paper presents a significant contribution to the field of continual learning. The combination of a novel architecture, a well-motivated self-activation mechanism, strong empirical results, and a focus on both forgetting and generalization makes it a valuable addition to the literature. Its memory view is also a welcome addition to better understanding adaptation in neural networks.
